| avoidance_of_hallucination | This criterion evaluates whether the answer introduces any false, fabricated, or unsupported visual  |
|                            | details not present in the image — including objects, attributes, actions, or spatial relationships. |
|                            | Hallucinations are penalized even if they appear plausible or are common in real life. Evaluation is |
|                            | done by cross-checking every claim in the answer against the visual evidence. For example, claiming  |
|                            | ‘a red umbrella is being held by the person’ when no umbrella is visible is a hallucination. This    |
|                            | criterion applies to all answers, regardless of question type, and is inapplicable when the answer   |
|                            | is purely interpretive or metaphorical (e.g., ‘the scene feels hopeful’) — but only if such          |
|                            | interpretations are visually grounded. Accuracy is measured by whether hallucinated content is       |
|                            | absent or explicitly flagged as uncertain.            |
|       clear_language       | This criterion evaluates whether the answer uses precise, unambiguous, and grammaticallycorrect     |
|                            | language that is easy to interpret without requiring additional context or inference. Itpenalizes   |
|                            | vague phrasing (e.g., ‘something’), jargon without explanation, grammatical errors, or stylistic     |
|                            | flourishes that obscure meaning. For example, ‘a cat is on the table’ is clearer than ‘afeline      |
|                            | entity rests upon the wooden surface’. Clear language is essential for both comprehension and        |
|                            | reliability — even if the content is factually correct, poor phrasing reduces trustworthiness. This  |
|                            | criterion applies to all visual question answering responses and is inapplicable when the answer is  |
|                            | intentionally poetic, metaphorical, or ambiguous by design (e.g., ‘the scene whispers mystery’), as  |
|                            | long as such ambiguity is visually grounded and does not mislead. Accuracy is measured by how        |
|                            | consistently the language conveys meaning without confusion or unnecessary ambiguity.            |

  warnings.warn(
+------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|    Criterion     | Description  |
+------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| visual_coherence | This criterion evaluates whether the answer’s spatial, compositional, or contextual relationships  |
|                  | between visually described elements are consistent with the spatial layout and relative positioning  |
|                  | of objects in the image. It applies only when the answer describes multiple objects, their relative  |
|                  | positions (e.g., ‘left of’, ‘behind’, ‘in front of’), or spatial configurations (e.g., ‘centered’,  |
|                  | ‘symmetrical’, ‘overlapping’). It does not apply to single-object descriptions or questions that do  |
|                  | not involve spatial reasoning. An answer is penalized if it misrepresents spatial relationships  |
|                  | (e.g., claiming ‘the cat is behind the dog’ when the dog is clearly behind the cat), or if it  |
|                  | invents spatial interactions not supported by the image. It also penalizes inconsistent framing or  |
|                  | perspective (e.g., describing an object as ‘on the left’ when it’s clearly centered from the  |
|                  | viewer’s perspective). The criterion is inapplicable when the answer is ambiguous about spatial  |
|                  | relationships or when the visual evidence is too occluded to verify position — in such cases,  |
|                  | calibrated uncertainty should be used instead. Evaluation is done by cross-checking every spatial  |
|                  | claim against the image’s layout, with preference given to the answer that aligns with visual  |
|                  | evidence. Unlike visual grounding, this criterion focuses specifically on relational and  |
|                  | compositional accuracy — not individual object identity or presence. It is distinct from  |
|                  | logical_consistency because it evaluates spatial truth, not internal contradictions. Workers must  |
|                  | prioritize visual evidence over semantic assumptions — for example, ‘the person is standing beside  |
|                  | the table’ is valid if visually confirmed, even if the table is partially obscured. This criterion  |
|                  | should not be applied to abstract or non-spatial questions, and should never override visual  |
|                  | grounding or factual consistency. The goal is to ensure spatial descriptions are not only accurate  |
|                  | but also contextually coherent within the image’s visual frame.  |
+------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

+----------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|      Criterion       | Description      |
+----------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|  stylistic_clarity   | This criterion evaluates whether the answer uses clear, unambiguous, and grammatically correct      |
|                      | language that minimizes interpretive ambiguity or stylistic vagueness — such as avoiding overly      |
|                      | poetic, metaphorical, or emotionally charged expressions unless they are explicitly grounded in      |
|                      | visual cues. It applies when the answer’s phrasing could confuse or mislead the reader (e.g.,      |
|                      | ‘mysterious glowing orb’ vs. ‘a red lightbulb’), or when stylistic choices obscure factual content.  |
|                      | It is inapplicable if the language is purely descriptive, factual, or visually grounded — and does   |
|                      | not override visual grounding or factual consistency. For example, ‘the cat is staring intensely’ is |
|                      | acceptable if the visual evidence supports intensity, but ‘the cat is a mystical guardian’ is      |
|                      | penalized for stylistic overreach. Workers must distinguish between stylistic enhancement and      |
|                      | hallucination — if a metaphor is visually supported (e.g., ‘balanced composition’ in a symmetrical   |
|                      | image), it is acceptable. This criterion is not about aesthetic quality or interpretive depth — only |
|                      | whether language is clear and unambiguous for the given question. Preference is given to the answer  |
|                      | with the clearest, most direct phrasing — even if less stylistically rich — as long as it remains    |
|                      | visually grounded. It is strictly secondary to visual grounding and factual consistency — if either  |
|                      | is violated, this criterion is inapplicable.      |
| semantic_specificity | This criterion evaluates whether the answer specifies the most precise, contextually relevant,and   |
|                      | visually verifiable details possible — avoiding generic or vague terms (e.g., ‘something’ or ‘a      |
|                      | person’) and favoring specific attributes (e.g., ‘a woman wearing a red jacket with a black hat’).   |
|                      | It applies when multiple visually distinguishable options exist (e.g., different objects, colors, or |
|                      | positions), and penalizes answers that omit or generalize visually distinct features. It is      |
|                      | inapplicable if the visual evidence is ambiguous or insufficient to support specificity — in which   |
|                      | case, calibrated uncertainty must be used. Specificity is evaluated by comparing the number and      |
|                      | precision of visually verifiable attributes described — higher specificity is preferred if both      |
|                      | answers are visually grounded and factually consistent. It does not penalize reasonable      |
|                      | generalizations if they are contextually justified and visually supported — e.g., ‘a red vehicle’ is |
|                      | acceptable if the color is visible and the object is clearly a vehicle. It does not override visual  |
|                      | grounding or factual consistency — if an answer is factually incorrect or visually ungrounded,      |
|                      | specificity is irrelevant. Workers must explicitly note inapplicability when visual evidence is      |
|                      | ambiguous or insufficient — and must not use this criterion to justify preferring a less specific    |
|                      | answer that is factually correct. This criterion is not about completeness — it is about precision   |
|                      | within the bounds of visual evidence. Example: ‘a dog sitting on the floor’ is less specific than ‘a |
|                      | black Labrador sitting on a wooden floor’ — if both are visually grounded, the more specific answer  |
|                      | is preferred.      |
| contextual_fidelity  | This criterion evaluates whether the answer’s description respects the visual context and narrative  |
|                      | implied by the image — such as background, lighting, environment, or implied interaction — without   |
|                      | fabricating or misrepresenting contextually relevant elements. It applies when the answer includes   |
|                      | contextual details (e.g., ‘indoor’, ‘daylight’, ‘on a street’, ‘with a crowd’) that are either      |
|                      | visually evident or logically implied by the image’s composition — and penalizes answers that ignore |
|                      | or contradict contextual cues (e.g., describing a night scene as ‘brightly lit’ or an indoor scene   |
|                      | as ‘outdoor’). It is inapplicable if the image lacks sufficient contextual cues (e.g., abstract art, |
|                      | heavily blurred scenes) — in which case, calibrated uncertainty must be used. Contextual fidelity is |
|                      | evaluated by comparing how accurately each answer reflects the image’s setting, lighting, or implied |
|                      | environment — preference is given to the answer that aligns with the visual context, even if it is   |
|                      | less specific or more general. It does not override visual grounding or factual consistency — if an  |
|                      | answer is factually incorrect or visually ungrounded, contextual fidelity is irrelevant. Workers     |
|                      | must explicitly note inapplicability when the visual context is ambiguous or insufficient — and must |
|                      | not use this criterion to justify preferring an answer that ignores visual context. This criterion   |
|                      | is not about aesthetic interpretation or stylistic enhancement — only whether the answer’s context   |
|                      | is visually or logically consistent with the image’s environment. Example: ‘a person walking in      |
|                      | bright daylight’ is contextually faithful if the image shows sunlight — ‘a person walking at night’  |
|                      | is contextually faithful if the image shows dark lighting — but ‘a person walking in a desert’is    |
|                      | contextually faithful only if the image shows desert features.      |

+------------------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|          Criterion           | Description              |
+------------------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| calibrated_uncertainty_usage | This criterion evaluates whether the answer appropriately uses calibrated uncertainty (e.g.,         |
|                              | ‘possibly’, ‘likely’, ‘appears to be’, ‘unclear’) when the visual evidence is ambiguous, incomplete, |
|                              | or insufficient to make a definitive claim. It applies only when the image lacks clearvisual        |
|                              | support for a specific assertion — such as occlusion, low resolution, or ambiguous object identity.  |
|                              | It penalizes answers that overstate certainty (e.g., ‘definitely a dog’ when the object is partially |
|                              | obscured) and rewards those that reflect uncertainty appropriately (e.g., ‘possibly a dog’, ‘unclear |
|                              | whether it’s a dog or a cat’). It is inapplicable when the visual evidence is unambiguous — in which |
|                              | case, the answer must be specific and confident. It does not override visual groundingor factual    |
|                              | consistency — if an answer is factually incorrect or visually ungrounded, this criterion is          |
|                              | irrelevant. The criterion is strictly secondary — it only evaluates the appropriateness of           |
|                              | uncertainty phrasing, not the truth of the claim. Accuracy is measured by whether the uncertainty    |
|                              | level matches the visual ambiguity — higher accuracy is given to answers that use uncertainty        |
|                              | precisely and avoid overconfidence. This criterion is not about stylistic tone or interpretive depth |
|                              | — only whether uncertainty is calibrated to the visual evidence.              |
|    multimodal_consistency    | This criterion evaluates whether the answer’s language is internally consistent with the visual      |
|                              | evidence — meaning the answer does not contradict itself or imply conflicting visual states (e.g.,   |
|                              | claiming ‘the door is open’ while also stating ‘the door is locked’), or misaligns with the visual   |
|                              | context (e.g., describing ‘a person walking’ while the visual shows ‘a person standingstill’). It   |
|                              | applies to all visual descriptions and must be evaluated independently of factual correctness or     |
|                              | biological plausibility — even if the answer is factually wrong, it may still be multimodally        |
|                              | consistent if it does not contradict itself or misrepresent the visual state. It is inapplicable if  |
|                              | the answer contains no claims, is empty, or is entirely hallucinated — in which case, it must be     |
|                              | evaluated under multimodal_alignment or avoidance_of_hallucination. The criterion is not about       |
|                              | semantic or contextual coherence — only whether the visual claims are logically consistent with each |
|                              | other and with the image. Preference is given to the answer that is internally consistent — even if  |
|                              | both are hallucinated — as long as one does not contradict the other. Workers must evaluate internal |
|                              | consistency first, then defer to visual grounding or factual consistency if a contradiction is       |
|                              | present. Accuracy is measured by whether the answer’s claims are logically consistent with the       |
|                              | visual evidence and with each other — not by truth or plausibility.              |

+-----------------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

| visual_ambiguity_resolution | This criterion evaluates whether the answer explicitly acknowledges or resolves visual ambiguities   |
|                             | present in the image — such as occlusion, low resolution, lighting shadows, or partial object        |
|                             | visibility — by either (1) explicitly stating uncertainty (e.g., ‘partially obscured’, ‘unclear due  |
|                             | to shadow’), (2) providing a plausible explanation for the ambiguity without hallucinating, or (3)   |
|                             | offering multiple interpretations with calibrated confidence. It applies only when the image         |
|                             | contains visual ambiguity that could affect object identification, spatial relationships, or         |
|                             | attribute description. It is inapplicable if the visual evidence is unambiguous or if the answer     |
|                             | makes no claim about the visual content. The criterion does not evaluate the correctness of the      |
|                             | resolution — only whether the ambiguity is acknowledged and handled with appropriate linguistic or   |
|                             | structural nuance. Preference is given to the answer that explicitly addresses ambiguity without     |
|                             | overconfident claims. It is distinct from ‘calibrated_uncertainty_usage’ — which evaluates           |
|                             | uncertainty phrasing in general — because this criterion specifically targets how ambiguity in the   |
|                             | visual input is acknowledged and resolved. It is also distinct from ‘visual_grounding’ or            |
|                             | ‘multimodal_alignment’, which evaluate object presence and visual fidelity, not ambiguity handling.  |
|                             | Workers must flag the criterion as inapplicable if the visual ambiguity is not present or if the     |
|                             | answer fails to acknowledge it. Accuracy is measured by whether the answer explicitly resolves or    |
|                             | acknowledges visual ambiguity in a non-hallucinatory, context-sensitive manner — not bythe          |
|                             | correctness of the interpretation.             |

|                                      |                                                    | reasonable contextual inferences (e.g., ‘preparing |
|                                      |                                                    | to throw’ based on a visible frisbee), or (c)      |
|                                      |                                                    | inferred motion or actionfrom posture — as long   |
|                                      |                                                    | as they are visually supported and explicitly      |
|                                      |                                                    | marked with uncertainty if ambiguous. The          |
|                                      |                                                    | criterion is secondary tovisual_grounding and     |
|                                      |                                                    | factual_consistency — if an answer is factually    |
|                                      |                                                    | incorrect or visually ungrounded, it may be        |
|                                      |                                                    | penalized under those criteria regardless of       |
|                                      |                                                    | alignment. Workers must prioritize literal visual  |
|                                      |                                                    | evidence — not interpretive or semantic            |
|                                      |                                                    | assumptions — and must reject any answer that      |
|                                      |                                                    | contains even one hallucinated claim, unless       |
|                                      |                                                    | explicitly qualified withuncertainty (which is    |
|                                      |                                                    | not allowed for hallucinations). Hallucinations    |
|                                      |                                                    | are only penalized for claims that fabricate or    |
|                                      |                                                    | misrepresent visual content — not for subjective   |
|                                      |                                                    | interpretations, semanticmislabeling (if visually |
|                                      |                                                    | identifiable), or reasonable contextual inferences |
|                                      |                                                    | — and workers must distinguish between             |
|                                      |                                                    | hallucination and factualmisstatement — with      |
|                                      |                                                    | factual misstatements penalized under              |
|                                      |                                                    | visual_grounding or factual_consistency. Claims    |
|                                      |                                                    | about text or numbers areonly hallucinated if not |
|                                      |                                                    | supported by visual evidence — and must not be     |
|                                      |                                                    | penalized for unverified claims unless they are    |
|                                      |                                                    | explicitly fabricated. Spatial dominance claims    |
|                                      |                                                    | like ‘small portion’ or ‘most of the space’ are    |
|                                      |                                                    | not hallucinations if visually supported — and     |
|                                      |                                                    | spatial positioning claims (e.g., ‘center’, ‘left  |
|                                      |                                                    | of’, ‘near the edge’) arenot hallucinations if    |
|                                      |                                                    | visually verifiable — with ‘center’ or ‘edge’      |
|                                      |                                                    | defined as literal, verifiable spatial claims.     |
|                                      |                                                    | Posture descriptions (e.g., ‘bent’, ‘flexed’,      |
|                                      |                                                    | ‘extended’) are not hallucinations if visually     |
|                                      |                                                    | supported — even if more detailed than generic     |
|                                      |                                                    | terms — as long as they are visually verifiable.   |
|                                      |                                                    | Semantic mislabeling (e.g., calling a silhouette   |
|                                      |                                                    | an ‘icon’) is permitted if the visual element is   |
|                                      |                                                    | present — and visual attributes (like shape,       |
|                                      |                                                    | pattern, silhouette) are not hallucinations unless |
|                                      |                                                    | they fabricate or misrepresent presence/absence.   |
|                                      |                                                    | The criterion must never penalize for visual       |
|                                      |                                                    | ambiguity — unless it leads to a fabricated claim  |
|                                      |                                                    | — and must require workers to assess whether the   |
|                                      |                                                    | claim is hallucinated or merely ambiguous — not    |
|                                      |                                                    | whether it is ‘more accurate’. Workers must be     |
|                                      |                                                    | instructed to use calibrated uncertainty for       |
|                                      |                                                    | ambiguous cases — not to penalize for lack of      |
|                                      |                                                    | certainty. The criterion must explicitly state     |
|                                      |                                                    | that it does not penalizefor semantic mislabeling |
|                                      |                                                    | — as long as the object is visually present — and  |
|                                      |                                                    | must provide clear examples of both allowed and    |
|                                      |                                                    | penalized claims to prevent misinterpretation by   |
|                                      |                                                    | workers. The criterion must emphasize that         |
|                                      |                                                    | hallucinations are only penalized for claims that  |
|                                      |                                                    | misrepresent or fabricatevisual content — not for |
|                                      |                                                    | non-visual claims — and must clearly distinguish   |

|                                      |                                                    | visual_grounding or factual_consistency. The       |

| action_or_state_accuracy | This criterion evaluates whether the answer correctly describes the action or state of visually      |
|                          | present entities — including static poses (e.g., ‘sitting’, ‘standing’, ‘leaning’), dynamic actions  |
|                          | (e.g., ‘jumping’, ‘falling’, ‘running’, ‘opening’, ‘closing’), or implied states (e.g., ‘sleeping’,  |
|                          | ‘resting’, ‘holding’). It applies ONLY when: (1) the answer makes a claim about an action or state,  |
|                          | (2) the entity is visually confirmed (via multimodal_alignment), and (3) the action/state is not     |
|                          | inferable solely from context or aesthetic interpretation — but must be visually verifiable through  |
|                          | posture, motion cues, object interaction, or implied motion. It is INAPPLICABLE if: (a) the claim is |
|                          | not about action or state (e.g., object identity, spatial layout, color, material), (b) the entity   |
|                          | is not visually confirmed, or (c) the claim is hallucinated or factually incorrect — in which case,  |
|                          | preference is determined by visual_grounding or multimodal_alignment first. The criterion does NOT   |
|                          | evaluate: (1) factual accuracy of object identity (handled by multimodal_alignment), (2) semantic or |
|                          | stylistic interpretations (e.g., ‘dramatic pose’, ‘posed for the camera’), (3) emotional or          |
|                          | personality states (e.g., ‘angry’, ‘calm’), or (4) inferred motion without visual cues (e.g.,        |
|                          | ‘possibly moving’ without posture or blur). It does NOT penalize: (a) cautious phrasing (e.g.,       |
|                          | ‘appears to be’, ‘possibly’) — if used appropriately — (b) plausible inferences grounded in visual   |
|                          | evidence, or (c) non-hallucinated additional context that does not misrepresent visual content.      |
|                          | Preference is given to the answer that accurately describes the action or state without overclaiming |
|                          | or introducing unsupported visual cues — such as motion blur or directional posture that contradicts |
|                          | the claim. If both answers are visually grounded, multimodal-aligned, and factually correct,         |
|                          | preference is given to the one with fewer or no factual misrepresentations of action or state — but  |
|                          | only if the claims are explicitly and visually verifiable. The criterion is strictly secondary to    |
|                          | visual_grounding and multimodal_alignment — if an answer fails those, it is disqualified regardless  |
|                          | of action accuracy. Workers must first verify visual_grounding and multimodal_alignment — and only   |
|                          | evaluate action_or_state_accuracy for answers that pass those criteria. If both answers are equally  |
|                          | accurate, defer to other criteria — never to subjective judgment. The criterion must not be used to  |
|                          | penalize accurate, specific claims that do not require action/state evaluation — only whenvisual    |
|                          | evidence supports a claim about action or state. If the criterion is inapplicable for bothanswers   |
|                          | (e.g., no action/state claims), defer to other criteria — never to subjective judgment. Importantly, |
|                          | action_or_state_accuracy cannot be used to evaluate or justify preference for hallucinatedor        |
|                          | misidentified claims — such answers are automatically disqualified under multimodal_alignment, and   |
|                          | the other answer is preferred without invoking this criterion. If both answers are inapplicable,     |
|                          | preference must not be assigned — the pair should be marked as a tie or deferred to other criteria   |
|                          | without defaulting to a tiebreaker. Workers must never assume or introduce tiebreakers — especially  |
|                          | arbitrary ones — when this criterion is inapplicable to both candidates. Preference is never given   |
|                          | to an answer that contains hallucinated action or state claims — even if it is more specific — and   |
|                          | this criterion is only used to break ties between equally grounded and factually correct answers.    |
|                          | Workers must evaluate visual grounding and multimodal_alignment first — and factual consistency is   |
|                          | only evaluated after those are verified — with action_or_state_accuracy being strictly secondary to  |
|                          | them. The criterion must emphasize that action or state claims are irrelevant if the answer is       |
|                          | factually wrong — and must be evaluated only after visual grounding and multimodal_alignment are     |
|                          | verified. Finally, the criterion must explicitly state that if both candidates are inapplicable,     |
|                          | preference must not be assigned — either the pair should be marked as a tie or the worker should be  |

#### Low
multimodal_alignment
+----------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|      Criterion       | Description      |
+----------------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| temporal_consistency | This criterion evaluates whether the answer’s description of motion, state changes, or implied      |
|                      | temporal progression is consistent with the static visual evidence — i.e., whether the answer      |
|                      | implies a sequence, duration, or transition that cannot be supported by the image (e.g., claiming    |
|                      | ‘the dog is running’ when the image shows a static pose, or ‘the person just opened the door’ when   |
|                      | the door is closed). It applies ONLY when: (1) the answer includes a claim about motion, state      |
|                      | change, or implied time sequence, and (2) the visual evidence is static (no motion blur, no temporal |
|                      | cues) — meaning no explicit or implicit indication of movement, action, or temporal progression is   |
|                      | visible. It is INAPPLICABLE if: (a) the claim is not about motion or temporal sequence, (b) the      |
|                      | visual evidence is dynamic (e.g., motion blur, action pose, temporal cues like a time stamp or      |
|                      | multiple frames), or (c) the claim is hallucinated or factually incorrect — in which case,      |
|                      | preference is determined by visual_grounding or multimodal_alignment first. The criterion doesNOT   |
|                      | evaluate: (1) static posture claims (e.g., ‘sitting’, ‘standing’ — handled by      |
|                      | action_or_state_accuracy), (2) factual accuracy of object identity or spatial layout (handled by     |
|                      | multimodal_alignment), (3) semantic or contextual inferences (e.g., ‘formal setting’), or (4)      |
|                      | inferred motion without visual cues (e.g., ‘possibly moving’ — handled by action_or_state_accuracy). |
|                      | It does NOT penalize: (a) cautious phrasing (e.g., ‘appears to be’, ‘possibly’) — if used      |
|                      | appropriately — (b) plausible inferences grounded in visual evidence, or (c) non-hallucinated      |
|                      | additional context that does not misrepresent visual content. Preference is given to the answer that |
|                      | avoids implying motion or temporal progression not supported by visual evidence — such as claiming   |
|                      | ‘the ball is rolling’ when the image shows a static ball. If both answers are visually grounded,     |
|                      | multimodal-aligned, and factually correct, preference is given to the one with fewer or no claims    |
|                      | implying unsupported temporal progression — but only if the claims are explicitly and visually      |
|                      | verifiable. The criterion is strictly secondary to visual_grounding and multimodal_alignment —if an |
|                      | answer fails those, it is disqualified regardless of temporal consistency. Workers must first verify |
|                      | visual_grounding and multimodal_alignment — and only evaluate temporal_consistency for answersthat  |
|                      | pass those criteria. If both answers are equally accurate, defer to other criteria — never to      |
|                      | subjective judgment. The criterion must not be used to penalize accurate, specific claims thatdo    |
|                      | not require temporal evaluation — only when visual evidence supports a claim about motion or      |
|                      | temporal progression. If the criterion is inapplicable for both answers (e.g., no motion or temporal |
|                      | claims), defer to other criteria — never to subjective judgment. Importantly, temporal_consistency   |
|                      | cannot be used to evaluate or justify preference for hallucinated or misidentified claims — such     |
|                      | answers are automatically disqualified under multimodal_alignment, and the other answer is preferred |
|                      | without invoking this criterion. If both answers are inapplicable, preference must not be assigned — |
|                      | the pair should be marked as a tie or deferred to other criteria without defaulting to a tiebreaker. |
|                      | Workers must never assume or introduce tiebreakers — especially arbitrary ones — when this criterion |
|                      | is inapplicable to both candidates. Preference is never given to an answer that contains      |
|                      | hallucinated temporal claims — even if it is more specific — and this criterion is only used to      |
|                      | break ties between equally grounded and factually correct answers. Workers must evaluate visual      |
|                      | grounding and multimodal_alignment first — and factual consistency is only evaluated after those are |
|                      | verified — with temporal_consistency being strictly secondary to them. The criterion must emphasize  |
|                      | that temporal claims are irrelevant if the answer is factually wrong — and must be evaluated only    |
|                      | after visual grounding and multimodal_alignment are verified. Finally, the criterion must explicitly |
|                      | state that if both candidates are inapplicable, preference must not be assigned — either the pair    |
|                      | should be marked as a tie or the worker should be instructed to defer to other criteria without      |
|                      | defaulting to the first option. The criterion must also clarify that it is never valid to assume or  |
|                      | introduce tiebreakers — especially arbitrary ones — when the criterion itself is inapplicable to     |
|                      | both candidates. The criterion must also avoid duplication with existing criteria — such as      |
|                      | action_or_state_accuracy (which evaluates motion/state in static or dynamic contexts) and      |
|                      | visual_coherence (which evaluates spatial relationships, not temporal progression).      |
